I woke up on Sunday morning at maybe 7:30, made my coffee—just my usual French roast, nothing fancy—and I was honestly so pumped to tackle it. I didn't feel sluggish or unmotivated in the slightest. \n\nI even drove all the way out to the Container Store right when they opened at 10 AM. Have you been there? It is dangerously easy to spend way too much money in that place! I bought all these clear acrylic bins, the ones with the little built-in label slots on the front, because I figured if I can see exactly what's in them, I won't lose my favorite wool hat again. I spent hours sorting everything, wiping down the shelves, making little labels. So the drive to do things is absolutely there, I promise you that. \n\nAnd it's the exact same with my hobbies. I have these little raised garden beds in my backyard, and I am already planning out what I'm going to plant this spring. I spent probably three hours on Pinterest the other night just looking up companion planting for tomatoes and marigolds, and I was making all these elaborate little sketches in my notebook of where everything is going to go. I am so excited to get out there in the dirt once the frost clears. \n\nSo, no, I never sit around feeling empty or like things don't matter. I'm always eager to jump into my projects or see my friends. It's really just the remembering part and the keeping track of the boring daily stuff that trips me up, not the wanting to do it part. I have all the motivation in the world!\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're sitting down trying to focus on one specific task—like reading an email from your boss or working through a problem at work—can you actually concentrate and pay attention to that one thing, or do you find your mind wandering or jumping around even when you're really trying to focus?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / secretary_or_administrative_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"At the office or in class, one phone ringing, people talking nearby, or even a random thought about something at home can pull me off what I was working on.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, my mind definitely jumps around. It is so hard to just lock in on one thing, no matter how hard I try. Like, okay, our office setup is probably part of the problem, I think, because my desk is right out in the open since I'm the first point of contact. But honestly, whether I'm at the office or, gosh, even when I was taking that evening continuing education class for QuickBooks a few months ago, it’s the exact same story. 

I will be sitting there, staring right at an email from my boss—and he writes these incredibly dense, multi-paragraph emails that you really can't skim, you have to read every single word—and I'll be trying so, so hard to focus on it. But then, just one phone ringing is all it takes. And it doesn't even have to be my phone! It could be Dave's phone all the way across the room, but the second it rings, my attention is just completely hijacked. I'll look up, I'll wonder who's calling him, I'll wonder if it's that difficult client we had last week... 

Or if there are people talking nearby... oh, forget about it. If Jessica and Tom are standing by the printer chatting about their weekend plans, I literally cannot tune them out. I’ll be looking at my computer monitor, nodding to myself like I'm reading, but my brain is one hundred percent focused on Tom’s story about his dog. 

And the funny thing is, it doesn't even have to be an actual, physical distraction in the room! Sometimes it’s just my own brain. I could be in a totally quiet space, right in the middle of working through a scheduling problem, and suddenly a random thought about something at home will just pop into my head out of nowhere. I'll be typing away and suddenly think, "Wait, did I remember to take the chicken out of the freezer?" or "I really need to measure that space in the hallway to see if a new shoe rack will fit." And just like that, I am completely pulled off what I was working on. I'll end up staring into space for five minutes before I realize I haven't read a single word of that email. It’s like my brain is a television and someone else has the remote, just constantly changing the channel on me when I’m trying to watch one specific show. So, yes, even when I am really, really trying to concentrate, I just get derailed so easily by almost anything.

And then I had to scramble and call the supplier and beg them to overnight it, which they did, thank goodness, but it was just an unnecessary headache, you know? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with personal errands. Like, last week I needed to pick up my winter coat from the dry cleaners. It's this little place right on my way home from work, I literally drive past it every single day. I didn't cry or feel guilty, I just let out this huge groan and gripped the steering wheel like, \"Why are you like this?!\" \n\nSo, yeah, it's not guilt. I know I'm a good person, and I know I'm a hard worker. I've always been a big reader anyway, mostly mysteries and thrillers, so words are definitely my strong suit, even with everything else feeling so scattered. \n\nAnd as for getting confused about where I am or what time it is... no, definitely not that either. I mean, I might look at the clock and realize, \"Oh shoot, I was supposed to make that phone call an hour ago,\" but it's not like I don't know that it's 3:00 PM on a Tuesday, you know? I always know exactly where I am. I've lived in the same area for years, and I take the same route to work every single day. I know every single pothole on 4th street! So I'm completely grounded in where I am and what day it is. \n\nAnd reading a map or organizing physical objects... actually, I'm really good at organizing physical things! That's the irony of all this. If you look at my desk at work, everything has a specific place. My pens are in the holder, my files are in this little tiered tray thing—the one I actually bought myself at Target last year because the office-supplied one was just too flimsy and it kept tipping over whenever I put more than three heavy folders in it. So physically putting things where they belong, or figuring out how to fit boxes into the supply closet so everything fits perfectly, I'm great at that. I have a really good eye for it. It's just... it's just remembering to actually *do* the tasks that's the problem, not the physical space or time or my words. It's so frustrating because my brain clearly works fine for all that other stuff you mentioned, it's just the memory and attention part that's letting me down.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 15 months since you started the evening classes, have you noticed changes in your mood? For instance, do you find yourself feeling persistently sad, down, or hopeless, or has your ability to enjoy things you normally like—like reading those mystery novels you mentioned—changed at all?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I definitely get frustrated with myself, like I said, because it's just so annoying to keep forgetting things, but I wouldn't say I feel sad or down or hopeless. It's more just... irritation? Like, I'll be sitting there and realize I forgot to email that spreadsheet to my boss, and I'll just think, \"Ugh, come on, get it together!\" But it's not a deep, dark sadness or anything like that. I don't wake up feeling miserable. I still look forward to things. \n\nLike you mentioned the reading—I still absolutely love my books. In fact, I just finished this amazing psychological thriller last week. It was one of those ones where you think you know exactly who did it by chapter three, but then they throw this massive twist at you right in the middle, and I ended up staying up until almost 2:00 AM just to finish it, which probably didn't help my focus the next day at work, if I'm being honest! But I just couldn't put it down. I love that feeling of being completely absorbed in a story. And I still enjoy going out to dinner with my friends on the weekends, or watching my favorite shows. \n\nSo, yeah, my mood is generally pretty fine. I mean, the classes are stressful, and work is busy, and the forgetting things makes everything feel a bit more overwhelming than it used to, but I'm not depressed or anything. I still laugh at the same jokes and enjoy my downtime just as much as I always have. It's just the day-to-day managing of all the little details that's got me pulling my hair out, not my actual mood or how I feel about life in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical workup or seen your primary care doctor about these memory and attention problems? And have you noticed any other physical changes in your health over these past 15 months—like headaches, sleep problems, fatigue, or anything else that feels different in your body?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about the primary care doctor, because I actually haven't gone to see her about this. I mean, I love Dr. Miller, she's been my doctor for, gosh, probably eight or nine years now. Her office is right by that little bakery on 5th Avenue, the one with the really good blueberry scones? But anyway, I just haven't made an appointment because, honestly, I keep thinking it's just me needing to try harder, or maybe I just need a better planner. I actually spent like two hours online last weekend looking at different planner layouts—daily versus weekly, vertical columns versus horizontal blocks—trying to figure out if maybe I just haven't found the right system yet. So, no, no medical workup, no blood tests or anything like that, because I just haven't brought it up to her. I guess I felt a little silly, like I should be able to manage a full-time job and a couple of evening classes without falling apart, you know? \n\nAnd as for physical changes... no, nothing really. I mean, you mentioned headaches, but I don't really get headaches. Maybe a slight one if I haven't had my morning coffee by 10:00 AM, but that's just normal caffeine withdrawal, right? Everyone gets that. But no weird, out-of-the-blue headaches or migraines or anything like that. \n\nSleep is... well, sleep is fine. I've always been a pretty solid sleeper. Like I said before, sometimes I stay up too late reading a really good book, but once I actually turn the light off and put the book down, I'm out like a light. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in the middle of the night staring at the ceiling. I usually get a solid seven or eight hours, unless I've done it to myself by staying up too late. And fatigue... no, not really. I mean, I'm tired at the end of the day, but it's a normal kind of tired. It's not like a bone-deep exhaustion or anything. It's just the regular \"I've been staring at a computer screen and answering phones all day and then sat through a two-hour lecture\" kind of tired. \n\nSo physically, my body feels totally normal. No weird aches, no changes in my appetite—I still love those blueberry scones a little too much!—no feeling weak or sick. It really is just my brain feeling like a sieve when it comes to keeping track of all the things I need to do. Everything from the neck down is working exactly the way it always has.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading one of those mystery novels you love, or spending time with friends on the weekends, do you find yourself feeling as engaged and interested as you used to, or does it feel like you have to push yourself a bit more to get into those activities these days?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, not at all! I don't have to push myself even a little bit. It's funny you ask that because just this past Saturday, my friends and I—Sarah, Jessica, and Brenda, we've been doing these little monthly get-togethers for, oh, it has to be five years now, ever since Brenda moved back from Chicago—we went to this new Italian place downtown. The one on the corner of Elm and 8th? Anyway, we were there for almost three hours just talking and laughing. Jessica was telling us about this absolute disaster of a blind date she went on, and I swear I was laughing so hard my sides actually hurt. I didn't feel checked out or like it was a chore to be there at all. I was completely in the moment, you know? \n\nAnd with my books... oh, it's the exact same. I don't have to force myself to open a book. If anything, I have to force myself to put it down! Like that thriller I mentioned before, the one with the crazy twist? When I was reading that, the rest of the world just completely faded away. I was so wrapped up in figuring out how the detective was going to catch the husband—because, I mean, it's always the husband, right? Well, usually, but this author really makes you doubt it for a second, she's so clever with her red herrings. But yeah, I was so invested. I even brought it with me to work in my tote bag just in case I had five minutes during my lunch break to read a few pages. I never actually get to, because the phones are always ringing off the hook, especially right around noon when everyone else is on their lunch break and decides that's the perfect time to call our office with a million questions. \n\nBut the point is, the desire to read is one hundred percent there. I never sit there staring at the page feeling bored or like I have to drag myself through a chapter. I'm totally glued to it. So no, I definitely don't feel disconnected or like I've lost my spark for the things I love. I'm just as interested in all my hobbies and my friends as I ever was! It's just the work and school stuff, the actual responsibilities, where my brain decides to take an unscheduled vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you forget to do these tasks at work or forget to run errands, do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself harshly, or does it feel more like just frustration with the situation itself?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely just frustration. I mean, I don't... I don't sit there and think I'm a horrible person or anything like that. It's not this deep, heavy guilt where I feel like a failure at life. It's really just pure, unadulterated annoyance with myself. \n\nLike, okay, for example, just last Tuesday at work. I'm a secretary, so keeping the office running smoothly is literally my entire job description, right? Well, we have this massive copier down the hall—it's one of those huge industrial ones that does the stapling and the hole punching and the sorting, but it takes this very specific, incredibly expensive toner that we have to special order. My boss asked me to order a backup cartridge on Monday morning because we were getting low. Well, Tuesday afternoon rolls around, the 'replace toner' light starts flashing red, and it just hit me like a ton of bricks. I completely forgot to place the order. \n\nBut when that happened, I didn't go into a spiral about it. I didn't feel worthless or hate myself. I was just really, really irritated. I literally slapped my forehead and went, \"Ugh, seriously? Come on, you knew you had to do this!\" I just felt silly. And then I had to scramble and call the supplier and beg them to overnight it, which they did, thank goodness, but it was just an unnecessary headache, you know? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with personal errands. Like, last week I needed to pick up my winter coat from the dry cleaners. It's this little place right on my way home from work, I literally drive past it every single day. I told myself all day, \"Don't forget the coat, don't forget the coat.\" And what did I do? I drove right past it, pulled into my driveway, turned off the car, and realized my back seat was empty. I didn't cry or feel guilty, I just let out this huge groan and gripped the steering wheel like, \"Why are you like this?!\" \n\nSo, yeah, it's not guilt. I know I'm a good person, and I know I'm a hard worker. I just get so incredibly frustrated that I can't seem to keep all the plates spinning right now without dropping one, especially when it's something so simple. I just wish I didn't have to constantly leave myself a million sticky notes—which half the time fall off my computer monitor anyway—just to remember basic things! But no, no harsh self-blame or anything dark like that. Just annoyance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling frustrated when you forget things, but I want to ask about your motivation more broadly. When you're not at work or in class, do you find yourself wanting to do things and getting things done, or do you sometimes feel like 'what's the point' or lack the drive to get started on tasks, even ones you know you'd normally enjoy or that need to be done?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I never get that sort of dark, heavy feeling where you just don't care or think, oh, what is the point of all this. Not at all. Actually, I have a million things I want to do! Like, just this past weekend, I decided I was going to completely overhaul my hall closet. It’s the one right by the front door where I keep all my winter gear, and it had become an absolute disaster zone—just a mountain of scarves and boots and mismatched gloves. I woke up on Sunday morning at maybe 7:30, made my coffee—just my usual French roast, nothing fancy—and I was honestly so pumped to tackle it. I didn't feel sluggish or unmotivated in the slightest. \n\nI even drove all the way out to the Container Store right when they opened at 10 AM. Have you been there? It is dangerously easy to spend way too much money in that place! I bought all these clear acrylic bins, the ones with the little built-in label slots on the front, because I figured if I can see exactly what's in them, I won't lose my favorite wool hat again. I spent hours sorting everything, wiping down the shelves, making little labels. So the drive to do things is absolutely there, I promise you that. \n\nAnd it's the exact same with my hobbies. I have these little raised garden beds in my backyard, and I am already planning out what I'm going to plant this spring. I spent probably three hours on Pinterest the other night just looking up companion planting for tomatoes and marigolds, and I was making all these elaborate little sketches in my notebook of where everything is going to go. I am so excited to get out there in the dirt once the frost clears. \n\nSo, no, I never sit around feeling empty or like things don't matter. I'm always eager to jump into my projects or see my friends. It's really just the remembering part and the keeping track of the boring daily stuff that trips me up, not the wanting to do it part. I have all the motivation in the world!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on one specific task—like reading an email from your boss or working through a problem at work—can you actually concentrate and pay attention to that one thing, or do you find your mind wandering or jumping around even when you're really trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mind definitely jumps around. It is so hard to just lock in on one thing, no matter how hard I try. Like, okay, our office setup is probably part of the problem, I think, because my desk is right out in the open since I'm the first point of contact. But honestly, whether I'm at the office or, gosh, even when I was taking that evening continuing education class for QuickBooks a few months ago, it’s the exact same story. \n\nI will be sitting there, staring right at an email from my boss—and he writes these incredibly dense, multi-paragraph emails that you really can't skim, you have to read every single word—and I'll be trying so, so hard to focus on it. But then, just one phone ringing is all it takes. And it doesn't even have to be my phone! It could be Dave's phone all the way across the room, but the second it rings, my attention is just completely hijacked. I'll look up, I'll wonder who's calling him, I'll wonder if it's that difficult client we had last week... \n\nOr if there are people talking nearby... oh, forget about it. If Jessica and Tom are standing by the printer chatting about their weekend plans, I literally cannot tune them out. I’ll be looking at my computer monitor, nodding to myself like I'm reading, but my brain is one hundred percent focused on Tom’s story about his dog. \n\nAnd the funny thing is, it doesn't even have to be an actual, physical distraction in the room! Sometimes it’s just my own brain. I could be in a totally quiet space, right in the middle of working through a scheduling problem, and suddenly a random thought about something at home will just pop into my head out of nowhere. I'll be typing away and suddenly think, \"Wait, did I remember to take the chicken out of the freezer?\" or \"I really need to measure that space in the hallway to see if a new shoe rack will fit.\" And just like that, I am completely pulled off what I was working on. I'll end up staring into space for five minutes before I realize I haven't read a single word of that email. It’s like my brain is a television and someone else has the remote, just constantly changing the channel on me when I’m trying to watch one specific show. So, yes, even when I am really, really trying to concentrate, I just get derailed so easily by almost anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, no, it’s definitely not worry. I’m not sitting there stressing out about the chicken or the shoe rack, you know? It’s not like my heart is racing or I’m panicking that dinner is going to be ruined or anything like that. It’s honestly just... random. Like, completely out of left field. 

I could be in the middle of typing up the minutes from the weekly staff meeting—which, by the way, takes forever because they always go off on these ridiculous tangent discussions about the breakroom fridge policy that I somehow have to summarize into professional bullet points—and right in the middle of typing, I’ll just suddenly think about... I don't know, the fact that I need to buy more of those little velvet hangers for my closet. 

And I’m not anxious about the hangers! I have plenty of hangers right now. It’s just a thought that literally drops into my brain from nowhere, almost like a pop-up ad on a website. You know when you're reading an article and suddenly there's an ad for shoes blocking the whole screen? You didn't click on anything to make it appear, it’s just there. And then once it’s there, I just kind of follow it. Like, oh, velvet hangers, I should check if Target has them on sale, maybe I’ll get the blush pink ones this time instead of the black ones because the black ones leave a little fuzz on my white blouses... and before I know it, I’m mentally reorganizing my wardrobe instead of finishing the meeting minutes. 

So, yeah, I'm never actually worried or anxious about the things that pop into my head. It’s more like my brain just gets bored with the email or the spreadsheet and decides to throw a totally random thought at me just to see what happens. It’s not this heavy, stressful thing at all. Just completely random noise!

Like, oh, velvet hangers, I should check if Target has them on sale, maybe I’ll get the blush pink ones this time instead of the black ones because the black ones leave a little fuzz on my white blouses... and before I know it, I’m mentally reorganizing my wardrobe instead of finishing the meeting minutes. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm never actually worried or anxious about the things that pop into my head. It’s more like my brain just gets bored with the email or the spreadsheet and decides to throw a totally random thought at me just to see what happens. It’s not this heavy, stressful thing at all. Just completely random noise!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Oh, absolutely. I definitely do that, especially when I'm stuck sitting in one place for an extended period. Like, going back to that evening QuickBooks class I was telling you about earlier—those classes were three hours long, twice a week, which is honestly just an eternity after you've already worked a full eight-hour shift at the front desk. And the chairs in that community college room were those terrible hard plastic ones that are attached to the little desks, you know the kind? They are just completely unforgiving on your lower back. 

Anyway, during those longer classes, I would always notice myself doing exactly what you're talking about. I’d be sitting there, really trying my absolute hardest to sit perfectly still and pay attention to the instructor—because QuickBooks is really not intuitive, let me tell you, and if you miss one step in the reconciliation process, the whole balance sheet is off—but my body just wouldn't cooperate. 

I'd catch myself clicking my pen over and over. Usually, I use these nice Pilot G2 gel pens, the retractable ones, and they have a very distinct, kind of loud click. The guy sitting next to me actually gave me a look once because I was doing it so much without even realizing it! And if I force myself to put the pen down and stop clicking it, then within two minutes I realize I'm bouncing my foot. Just rapidly tapping my heel on the linoleum floor under the desk. 

And the shifting! I am constantly shifting around in the chair. I’ll cross my right leg over my left, then a few minutes later I just have to uncross them and cross the left over the right, then I’m leaning on my left elbow, then I’m sitting up straight, then I’m slouching again. It’s like my body just physically rejects the idea of staying in one position. Even when I am genuinely trying to sit still and focus, I just can't seem to stop the little movements. I don't necessarily feel this overwhelming, bursting urge to get up and run a marathon or anything, it's not like I need to pace the hallways, but it's just this constant, low-level need to be doing *something* with my hands or my feet. Even at my desk at work, if I'm on a long phone call with a vendor, I'm usually swiveling side to side in my office chair or unbending a paperclip while I talk. I just can't seem to stay totally still!

Well, Tuesday afternoon rolls around, the 'replace toner' light starts flashing red, and it just hit me like a ton of bricks. I completely forgot to place the order. \n\nBut when that happened, I didn't go into a spiral about it. I didn't feel worthless or hate myself. I was just really, really irritated. I literally slapped my forehead and went, \"Ugh, seriously? Come on, you knew you had to do this!\" I just felt silly. And then I had to scramble and call the supplier and beg them to overnight it, which they did, thank goodness, but it was just an unnecessary headache, you know? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with personal errands. Like, last week I needed to pick up my winter coat from the dry cleaners. It's this little place right on my way home from work, I literally drive past it every single day. I told myself all day, \"Don't forget the coat, don't forget the coat.\" And what did I do? I drove right past it, pulled into my driveway, turned off the car, and realized my back seat was empty. I didn't cry or feel guilty, I just let out this huge groan and gripped the steering wheel like, \"Why are you like this?!\" \n\nSo, yeah, it's not guilt. I know I'm a good person, and I know I'm a hard worker. I just get so incredibly frustrated that I can't seem to keep all the plates spinning right now without dropping one, especially when it's something so simple. I just wish I didn't have to constantly leave myself a million sticky notes—which half the time fall off my computer monitor anyway—just to remember basic things! But no, no harsh self-blame or anything dark like that. Just annoyance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling frustrated when you forget things, but I want to ask about your motivation more broadly. When you're not at work or in class, do you find yourself wanting to do things and getting things done, or do you sometimes feel like 'what's the point' or lack the drive to get started on tasks, even ones you know you'd normally enjoy or that need to be done?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I never get that sort of dark, heavy feeling where you just don't care or think, oh, what is the point of all this. Not at all. Actually, I have a million things I want to do! Like, just this past weekend, I decided I was going to completely overhaul my hall closet. It’s the one right by the front door where I keep all my winter gear, and it had become an absolute disaster zone—just a mountain of scarves and boots and mismatched gloves. I woke up on Sunday morning at maybe 7:30, made my coffee—just my usual French roast, nothing fancy—and I was honestly so pumped to tackle it. I didn't feel sluggish or unmotivated in the slightest. \n\nI even drove all the way out to the Container Store right when they opened at 10 AM. Have you been there? It is dangerously easy to spend way too much money in that place! I bought all these clear acrylic bins, the ones with the little built-in label slots on the front, because I figured if I can see exactly what's in them, I won't lose my favorite wool hat again. I spent hours sorting everything, wiping down the shelves, making little labels. So the drive to do things is absolutely there, I promise you that. \n\nAnd it's the exact same with my hobbies. I have these little raised garden beds in my backyard, and I am already planning out what I'm going to plant this spring. I spent probably three hours on Pinterest the other night just looking up companion planting for tomatoes and marigolds, and I was making all these elaborate little sketches in my notebook of where everything is going to go. I am so excited to get out there in the dirt once the frost clears. \n\nSo, no, I never sit around feeling empty or like things don't matter. I'm always eager to jump into my projects or see my friends. It's really just the remembering part and the keeping track of the boring daily stuff that trips me up, not the wanting to do it part. I have all the motivation in the world!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on one specific task—like reading an email from your boss or working through a problem at work—can you actually concentrate and pay attention to that one thing, or do you find your mind wandering or jumping around even when you're really trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mind definitely jumps around. It is so hard to just lock in on one thing, no matter how hard I try. Like, okay, our office setup is probably part of the problem, I think, because my desk is right out in the open since I'm the first point of contact. But honestly, whether I'm at the office or, gosh, even when I was taking that evening continuing education class for QuickBooks a few months ago, it’s the exact same story. \n\nI will be sitting there, staring right at an email from my boss—and he writes these incredibly dense, multi-paragraph emails that you really can't skim, you have to read every single word—and I'll be trying so, so hard to focus on it. But then, just one phone ringing is all it takes. And it doesn't even have to be my phone! It could be Dave's phone all the way across the room, but the second it rings, my attention is just completely hijacked. I'll look up, I'll wonder who's calling him, I'll wonder if it's that difficult client we had last week... \n\nOr if there are people talking nearby... oh, forget about it. If Jessica and Tom are standing by the printer chatting about their weekend plans, I literally cannot tune them out. I’ll be looking at my computer monitor, nodding to myself like I'm reading, but my brain is one hundred percent focused on Tom’s story about his dog. \n\nAnd the funny thing is, it doesn't even have to be an actual, physical distraction in the room! Sometimes it’s just my own brain. I could be in a totally quiet space, right in the middle of working through a scheduling problem, and suddenly a random thought about something at home will just pop into my head out of nowhere. I'll be typing away and suddenly think, \"Wait, did I remember to take the chicken out of the freezer?\" or \"I really need to measure that space in the hallway to see if a new shoe rack will fit.\" And just like that, I am completely pulled off what I was working on. I'll end up staring into space for five minutes before I realize I haven't read a single word of that email. It’s like my brain is a television and someone else has the remote, just constantly changing the channel on me when I’m trying to watch one specific show. So, yes, even when I am really, really trying to concentrate, I just get derailed so easily by almost anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something and your mind wanders to random thoughts like the chicken in the freezer or the shoe rack—do those thoughts feel like they're just popping up randomly and grabbing your attention, or do they feel more like you're actively worried or anxious about those things and can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s definitely not worry. I’m not sitting there stressing out about the chicken or the shoe rack, you know? It’s not like my heart is racing or I’m panicking that dinner is going to be ruined or anything like that. It’s honestly just... random. Like, completely out of left field. \n\nI could be in the middle of typing up the minutes from the weekly staff meeting—which, by the way, takes forever because they always go off on these ridiculous tangent discussions about the breakroom fridge policy that I somehow have to summarize into professional bullet points—and right in the middle of typing, I’ll just suddenly think about... I don't know, the fact that I need to buy more of those little velvet hangers for my closet. \n\nAnd I’m not anxious about the hangers! I have plenty of hangers right now. It’s just a thought that literally drops into my brain from nowhere, almost like a pop-up ad on a website. You know when you're reading an article and suddenly there's an ad for shoes blocking the whole screen? You didn't click on anything to make it appear, it’s just there. And then once it’s there, I just kind of follow it. Like, oh, velvet hangers, I should check if Target has them on sale, maybe I’ll get the blush pink ones this time instead of the black ones because the black ones leave a little fuzz on my white blouses... and before I know it, I’m mentally reorganizing my wardrobe instead of finishing the meeting minutes. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm never actually worried or anxious about the things that pop into my head. It’s more like my brain just gets bored with the email or the spreadsheet and decides to throw a totally random thought at me just to see what happens. It’s not this heavy, stressful thing at all. Just completely random noise!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your desk trying to work, or even when you're just relaxing at home, do you find yourself fidgeting a lot—like tapping your pen, bouncing your leg, or just having trouble sitting still—or do you feel restless and like you need to be moving around?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Usually, I use these nice Pilot G2 gel pens, the retractable ones, and they have a very distinct, kind of loud click. The guy sitting next to me actually gave me a look once because I was doing it so much without even realizing it! And if I force myself to put the pen down and stop clicking it, then within two minutes I realize I'm bouncing my foot. Just rapidly tapping my heel on the linoleum floor under the desk. \n\nAnd the shifting! I am constantly shifting around in the chair. I’ll cross my right leg over my left, then a few minutes later I just have to uncross them and cross the left over the right, then I’m leaning on my left elbow, then I’m sitting up straight, then I’m slouching again. It’s like my body just physically rejects the idea of staying in one position. Even when I am genuinely trying to sit still and focus, I just can't seem to stop the little movements. I don't necessarily feel this overwhelming, bursting urge to get up and run a marathon or anything, it's not like I need to pace the hallways, but it's just this constant, low-level need to be doing *something* with my hands or my feet. Even at my desk at work, if I'm on a long phone call with a vendor, I'm usually swiveling side to side in my office chair or unbending a paperclip while I talk. I just can't seem to stay totally still!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Crucially, mood is normal (no depression), motivation is intact, no anxiety about intrusive thoughts, good engagement in hobbies and social activities, and no cognitive deficits (language, visuospatial, orientation). I could be in a totally quiet space, right in the middle of working through a scheduling problem, and suddenly a random thought about something at home will just pop into my head out of nowhere. I'll end up staring into space for five minutes before I realize I haven't read a single word of that email. It’s like my brain is a television and someone else has the remote, just constantly changing the channel on me when I’m trying to watch one specific show. So, yes, even when I am really, really trying to concentrate, I just get derailed so easily by almost anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something and your mind wanders to random thoughts like the chicken in the freezer or the shoe rack—do those thoughts feel like they're just popping up randomly and grabbing your attention, or do they feel more like you're actively worried or anxious about those things and can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s definitely not worry. I’m not sitting there stressing out about the chicken or the shoe rack, you know? It’s not like my heart is racing or I’m panicking that dinner is going to be ruined or anything like that. It’s honestly just... random. Like, completely out of left field. \n\nI could be in the middle of typing up the minutes from the weekly staff meeting—which, by the way, takes forever because they always go off on these ridiculous tangent discussions about the breakroom fridge policy that I somehow have to summarize into professional bullet points—and right in the middle of typing, I’ll just suddenly think about... I don't know, the fact that I need to buy more of those little velvet hangers for my closet. \n\nAnd I’m not anxious about the hangers! I have plenty of hangers right now. It’s just a thought that literally drops into my brain from nowhere, almost like a pop-up ad on a website. You know when you're reading an article and suddenly there's an ad for shoes blocking the whole screen? You didn't click on anything to make it appear, it’s just there. And then once it’s there, I just kind of follow it. Like, oh, velvet hangers, I should check if Target has them on sale, maybe I’ll get the blush pink ones this time instead of the black ones because the black ones leave a little fuzz on my white blouses... and before I know it, I’m mentally reorganizing my wardrobe instead of finishing the meeting minutes. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm never actually worried or anxious about the things that pop into my head. It’s more like my brain just gets bored with the email or the spreadsheet and decides to throw a totally random thought at me just to see what happens. It’s not this heavy, stressful thing at all. Just completely random noise!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your desk trying to work, or even when you're just relaxing at home, do you find yourself fidgeting a lot—like tapping your pen, bouncing your leg, or just having trouble sitting still—or do you feel restless and like you need to be moving around?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I definitely do that, especially when I'm stuck sitting in one place for an extended period. Like, going back to that evening QuickBooks class I was telling you about earlier—those classes were three hours long, twice a week, which is honestly just an eternity after you've already worked a full eight-hour shift at the front desk. And the chairs in that community college room were those terrible hard plastic ones that are attached to the little desks, you know the kind? They are just completely unforgiving on your lower back. \n\nAnyway, during those longer classes, I would always notice myself doing exactly what you're talking about. I’d be sitting there, really trying my absolute hardest to sit perfectly still and pay attention to the instructor—because QuickBooks is really not intuitive, let me tell you, and if you miss one step in the reconciliation process, the whole balance sheet is off—but my body just wouldn't cooperate. \n\nI'd catch myself clicking my pen over and over. Usually, I use these nice Pilot G2 gel pens, the retractable ones, and they have a very distinct, kind of loud click. The guy sitting next to me actually gave me a look once because I was doing it so much without even realizing it! And if I force myself to put the pen down and stop clicking it, then within two minutes I realize I'm bouncing my foot. Just rapidly tapping my heel on the linoleum floor under the desk. \n\nAnd the shifting! I am constantly shifting around in the chair. I’ll cross my right leg over my left, then a few minutes later I just have to uncross them and cross the left over the right, then I’m leaning on my left elbow, then I’m sitting up straight, then I’m slouching again. It’s like my body just physically rejects the idea of staying in one position. Even when I am genuinely trying to sit still and focus, I just can't seem to stop the little movements. I don't necessarily feel this overwhelming, bursting urge to get up and run a marathon or anything, it's not like I need to pace the hallways, but it's just this constant, low-level need to be doing *something* with my hands or my feet. Even at my desk at work, if I'm on a long phone call with a vendor, I'm usually swiveling side to side in my office chair or unbending a paperclip while I talk. I just can't seem to stay totally still!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow, yes. Going all the way back to elementary school, it was... well, it was a whole ordeal, honestly. My mom and I actually joke about it now, but back then it was incredibly frustrating for her. I don't know if my teachers specifically used the word fidgeting on my report cards, but they definitely noticed I was struggling to keep things together and stay on track. 

I was absolutely that kid who lost her homework in her backpack. I had this bright purple Lisa Frank backpack—the one with the neon dolphins, I loved that thing so much—but the inside of it was just a complete disaster zone. It was like a black hole. I would do my math worksheet at the kitchen table, put it in my folder, put the folder in the backpack, and then somehow by nine in the morning the next day, it was just gone. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be digging through old wrappers, broken crayons, and just mounds of crumpled paper at the bottom of the bag, and I just couldn't find it. 

And permission slips! Oh my gosh, forget about it. I was notorious for forgetting permission slips. We would have these field trips, like I remember this one specific trip to the natural history museum in third grade, and the teacher had handed out the bright yellow slips a week in advance. I stuffed it in my desk, or my bag, and completely forgot it existed until the morning we were supposed to get on the bus. My mom actually had to leave her job, drive all the way to the school, and sign a spare copy in the front office so I wouldn't have to stay behind in the library. She would always find the original slip like three weeks later, completely crinkled up under a squished juice box.

It was the exact same story with any kind of assignment, too. If we had a project, I just couldn't stay focused on it. I needed my mother, or my teacher—like Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, she was such a saint—they literally had to remind me step by step to finish projects. If they just told me to build a diorama by Friday, I would stare at the shoebox and get completely overwhelmed or distracted by the glue sticks. My mom would have to sit right next to me and tell me to paint the background first, and then tell me to wait for it to dry, and then remind me to cut out the trees. So, yeah, this definitely isn't a new thing for me, it's been happening since I was little.

Or I'll have a simple registration form that I just need to sign and turn in, and I'll leave it sitting right there on the kitchen counter for days and just... totally blank on actually taking it with me. \n\nAnd it’s happening with my personal errands, too. I'll literally pull into my driveway, put the car in park, and then realize I completely forgot to stop for the errand. It’s just these little daily things, but it’s happening so much and it's so unlike how I usually am, that I just felt like I had to talk to someone about it before I end up getting in trouble at work or something.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these memory and attention problems? Did they come on suddenly over days or weeks, or have they been gradually getting worse over months?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about the timeline, because I've actually spent a lot of time thinking about exactly that. At first, I thought maybe I was just tired, you know? But if I really sit down and trace it back, it hasn't been a sudden thing at all. It didn't just happen overnight or even over a few weeks. It's been more of a slow build, but I can actually pinpoint when it started to become a real, noticeable issue for me. \n\nIt was about 15 months ago. I remember because that's when I decided to go back to school—well, not a full degree, but an evening certificate program to maybe help me move up at work. I was so excited about it. I bought all these new notebooks and highlighters, and I had this whole color-coded system planned out for my schedule between work and classes. But almost as soon as the program started, it was like my brain just couldn't hold all the pieces together anymore. I thought, \"Okay, this is just an adjustment period,\" but it never really got better. The attention issues, the struggling to keep things organized... it all just became noticeably harder to manage right around that 15-month mark when I added the evening classes to my plate. And it's just been this constant, exhausting effort ever since then to try and keep my head above water with everything.\n\nDoctor: Besides the memory and attention problems, have you noticed any other changes in how your mind works—like difficulty finding the right words, getting confused about where you are or what time it is, or trouble with things like reading a map or organizing objects in space?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, nothing like that. Honestly, my vocabulary and talking to people, that's never been a problem. In my job, I have to draft emails all day and talk to clients, and I always know exactly what I want to say. Well, sometimes I might type a little fast and make a typo, but finding the actual words in my head? No, that's completely fine. I've always been a big reader anyway, mostly mysteries and thrillers, so words are definitely my strong suit, even with everything else feeling so scattered. \n\nAnd as for getting confused about where I am or what time it is... no, definitely not that either. I mean, I might look at the clock and realize, \"Oh shoot, I was supposed to make that phone call an hour ago,\" but it's not like I don't know that it's 3:00 PM on a Tuesday, you know? I always know exactly where I am. I've lived in the same area for years, and I take the same route to work every single day. I know every single pothole on 4th street! So I'm completely grounded in where I am and what day it is. \n\nAnd reading a map or organizing physical objects... actually, I'm really good at organizing physical things! That's the irony of all this. If you look at my desk at work, everything has a specific place. My pens are in the holder, my files are in this little tiered tray thing—the one I actually bought myself at Target last year because the office-supplied one was just too flimsy and it kept tipping over whenever I put more than three heavy folders in it. So physically putting things where they belong, or figuring out how to fit boxes into the supply closet so everything fits perfectly, I'm great at that. I have a really good eye for it. It's just... it's just remembering to actually *do* the tasks that's the problem, not the physical space or time or my words. It's so frustrating because my brain clearly works fine for all that other stuff you mentioned, it's just the memory and attention part that's letting me down.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 15 months since you started the evening classes, have you noticed changes in your mood? For instance, do you find yourself feeling persistently sad, down, or hopeless, or has your ability to enjoy things you normally like—like reading those mystery novels you mentioned—changed at all?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I definitely get frustrated with myself, like I said, because it's just so annoying to keep forgetting things, but I wouldn't say I feel sad or down or hopeless. It's more just... irritation? Like, I'll be sitting there and realize I forgot to email that spreadsheet to my boss, and I'll just think, \"Ugh, come on, get it together!\" But it's not a deep, dark sadness or anything like that. I don't wake up feeling miserable. I still look forward to things. \n\nLike you mentioned the reading—I still absolutely love my books. In fact, I just finished this amazing psychological thriller last week. It was one of those ones where you think you know exactly who did it by chapter three, but then they throw this massive twist at you right in the middle, and I ended up staying up until almost 2:00 AM just to finish it, which probably didn't help my focus the next day at work, if I'm being honest! But I just couldn't put it down. I love that feeling of being completely absorbed in a story. And I still enjoy going out to dinner with my friends on the weekends, or watching my favorite shows. \n\nSo, yeah, my mood is generally pretty fine. I mean, the classes are stressful, and work is busy, and the forgetting things makes everything feel a bit more overwhelming than it used to, but I'm not depressed or anything. I still laugh at the same jokes and enjoy my downtime just as much as I always have. It's just the day-to-day managing of all the little details that's got me pulling my hair out, not my actual mood or how I feel about life in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical workup or seen your primary care doctor about these memory and attention problems? And have you noticed any other physical changes in your health over these past 15 months—like headaches, sleep problems, fatigue, or anything else that feels different in your body?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about the primary care doctor, because I actually haven't gone to see her about this. I mean, I love Dr. Miller, she's been my doctor for, gosh, probably eight or nine years now. Her office is right by that little bakery on 5th Avenue, the one with the really good blueberry scones? But anyway, I just haven't made an appointment because, honestly, I keep thinking it's just me needing to try harder, or maybe I just need a better planner. I actually spent like two hours online last weekend looking at different planner layouts—daily versus weekly, vertical columns versus horizontal blocks—trying to figure out if maybe I just haven't found the right system yet. So, no, no medical workup, no blood tests or anything like that, because I just haven't brought it up to her. I guess I felt a little silly, like I should be able to manage a full-time job and a couple of evening classes without falling apart, you know? \n\nAnd as for physical changes... no, nothing really. I mean, you mentioned headaches, but I don't really get headaches. Maybe a slight one if I haven't had my morning coffee by 10:00 AM, but that's just normal caffeine withdrawal, right? Everyone gets that. But no weird, out-of-the-blue headaches or migraines or anything like that. \n\nSleep is... well, sleep is fine. I've always been a pretty solid sleeper. Like I said before, sometimes I stay up too late reading a really good book, but once I actually turn the light off and put the book down, I'm out like a light. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in the middle of the night staring at the ceiling. I usually get a solid seven or eight hours, unless I've done it to myself by staying up too late. And fatigue... no, not really. I mean, I'm tired at the end of the day, but it's a normal kind of tired. It's not like a bone-deep exhaustion or anything. It's just the regular \"I've been staring at a computer screen and answering phones all day and then sat through a two-hour lecture\" kind of tired. \n\nSo physically, my body feels totally normal. No weird aches, no changes in my appetite—I still love those blueberry scones a little too much!—no feeling weak or sick. It really is just my brain feeling like a sieve when it comes to keeping track of all the things I need to do. Everything from the neck down is working exactly the way it always has.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading one of those mystery novels you love, or spending time with friends on the weekends, do you find yourself feeling as engaged and interested as you used to, or does it feel like you have to push yourself a bit more to get into those activities these days?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, not at all! I don't have to push myself even a little bit. It's funny you ask that because just this past Saturday, my friends and I—Sarah, Jessica, and Brenda, we've been doing these little monthly get-togethers for, oh, it has to be five years now, ever since Brenda moved back from Chicago—we went to this new Italian place downtown. The one on the corner of Elm and 8th? Anyway, we were there for almost three hours just talking and laughing. Jessica was telling us about this absolute disaster of a blind date she went on, and I swear I was laughing so hard my sides actually hurt. I didn't feel checked out or like it was a chore to be there at all. I was completely in the moment, you know? \n\nAnd with my books... oh, it's the exact same. I don't have to force myself to open a book. If anything, I have to force myself to put it down! Like that thriller I mentioned before, the one with the crazy twist? When I was reading that, the rest of the world just completely faded away. I was so wrapped up in figuring out how the detective was going to catch the husband—because, I mean, it's always the husband, right? Well, usually, but this author really makes you doubt it for a second, she's so clever with her red herrings. But yeah, I was so invested. I even brought it with me to work in my tote bag just in case I had five minutes during my lunch break to read a few pages. I never actually get to, because the phones are always ringing off the hook, especially right around noon when everyone else is on their lunch break and decides that's the perfect time to call our office with a million questions. \n\nBut the point is, the desire to read is one hundred percent there. I never sit there staring at the page feeling bored or like I have to drag myself through a chapter. I'm totally glued to it. So no, I definitely don't feel disconnected or like I've lost my spark for the things I love. I'm just as interested in all my hobbies and my friends as I ever was! It's just the work and school stuff, the actual responsibilities, where my brain decides to take an unscheduled vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you forget to do these tasks at work or forget to run errands, do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself harshly, or does it feel more like just frustration with the situation itself?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely just frustration. I mean, I don't... I don't sit there and think I'm a horrible person or anything like that. It's not this deep, heavy guilt where I feel like a failure at life. It's really just pure, unadulterated annoyance with myself. \n\nLike, okay, for example, just last Tuesday at work. I'm a secretary, so keeping the office running smoothly is literally my entire job description, right? Well, we have this massive copier down the hall—it's one of those huge industrial ones that does the stapling and the hole punching and the sorting, but it takes this very specific, incredibly expensive toner that we have to special order. My boss asked me to order a backup cartridge on Monday morning because we were getting low. Well, Tuesday afternoon rolls around, the 'replace toner' light starts flashing red, and it just hit me like a ton of bricks. I completely forgot to place the order. \n\nBut when that happened, I didn't go into a spiral about it. I didn't feel worthless or hate myself. I was just really, really irritated. I literally slapped my forehead and went, \"Ugh, seriously? Come on, you knew you had to do this!\" I just felt silly. And then I had to scramble and call the supplier and beg them to overnight it, which they did, thank goodness, but it was just an unnecessary headache, you know? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with personal errands. Like, last week I needed to pick up my winter coat from the dry cleaners. It's this little place right on my way home from work, I literally drive past it every single day. I told myself all day, \"Don't forget the coat, don't forget the coat.\" And what did I do? I drove right past it, pulled into my driveway, turned off the car, and realized my back seat was empty. I didn't cry or feel guilty, I just let out this huge groan and gripped the steering wheel like, \"Why are you like this?!\" \n\nSo, yeah, it's not guilt. I know I'm a good person, and I know I'm a hard worker. I just get so incredibly frustrated that I can't seem to keep all the plates spinning right now without dropping one, especially when it's something so simple. I just wish I didn't have to constantly leave myself a million sticky notes—which half the time fall off my computer monitor anyway—just to remember basic things! But no, no harsh self-blame or anything dark like that. Just annoyance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling frustrated when you forget things, but I want to ask about your motivation more broadly. When you're not at work or in class, do you find yourself wanting to do things and getting things done, or do you sometimes feel like 'what's the point' or lack the drive to get started on tasks, even ones you know you'd normally enjoy or that need to be done?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I never get that sort of dark, heavy feeling where you just don't care or think, oh, what is the point of all this. Not at all. Actually, I have a million things I want to do! Like, just this past weekend, I decided I was going to completely overhaul my hall closet. It’s the one right by the front door where I keep all my winter gear, and it had become an absolute disaster zone—just a mountain of scarves and boots and mismatched gloves. I woke up on Sunday morning at maybe 7:30, made my coffee—just my usual French roast, nothing fancy—and I was honestly so pumped to tackle it. I didn't feel sluggish or unmotivated in the slightest. \n\nI even drove all the way out to the Container Store right when they opened at 10 AM. Have you been there? It is dangerously easy to spend way too much money in that place! I bought all these clear acrylic bins, the ones with the little built-in label slots on the front, because I figured if I can see exactly what's in them, I won't lose my favorite wool hat again. I spent hours sorting everything, wiping down the shelves, making little labels. So the drive to do things is absolutely there, I promise you that. \n\nAnd it's the exact same with my hobbies. I have these little raised garden beds in my backyard, and I am already planning out what I'm going to plant this spring. I spent probably three hours on Pinterest the other night just looking up companion planting for tomatoes and marigolds, and I was making all these elaborate little sketches in my notebook of where everything is going to go. I am so excited to get out there in the dirt once the frost clears. \n\nSo, no, I never sit around feeling empty or like things don't matter. I'm always eager to jump into my projects or see my friends. It's really just the remembering part and the keeping track of the boring daily stuff that trips me up, not the wanting to do it part. I have all the motivation in the world!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on one specific task—like reading an email from your boss or working through a problem at work—can you actually concentrate and pay attention to that one thing, or do you find your mind wandering or jumping around even when you're really trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mind definitely jumps around. It is so hard to just lock in on one thing, no matter how hard I try. Like, okay, our office setup is probably part of the problem, I think, because my desk is right out in the open since I'm the first point of contact. But honestly, whether I'm at the office or, gosh, even when I was taking that evening continuing education class for QuickBooks a few months ago, it’s the exact same story. \n\nI will be sitting there, staring right at an email from my boss—and he writes these incredibly dense, multi-paragraph emails that you really can't skim, you have to read every single word—and I'll be trying so, so hard to focus on it. But then, just one phone ringing is all it takes. And it doesn't even have to be my phone! It could be Dave's phone all the way across the room, but the second it rings, my attention is just completely hijacked. I'll look up, I'll wonder who's calling him, I'll wonder if it's that difficult client we had last week... \n\nOr if there are people talking nearby... oh, forget about it. If Jessica and Tom are standing by the printer chatting about their weekend plans, I literally cannot tune them out. I’ll be looking at my computer monitor, nodding to myself like I'm reading, but my brain is one hundred percent focused on Tom’s story about his dog. \n\nAnd the funny thing is, it doesn't even have to be an actual, physical distraction in the room! Sometimes it’s just my own brain. I could be in a totally quiet space, right in the middle of working through a scheduling problem, and suddenly a random thought about something at home will just pop into my head out of nowhere. I'll be typing away and suddenly think, \"Wait, did I remember to take the chicken out of the freezer?\" or \"I really need to measure that space in the hallway to see if a new shoe rack will fit.\" And just like that, I am completely pulled off what I was working on. I'll end up staring into space for five minutes before I realize I haven't read a single word of that email. It’s like my brain is a television and someone else has the remote, just constantly changing the channel on me when I’m trying to watch one specific show. So, yes, even when I am really, really trying to concentrate, I just get derailed so easily by almost anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something and your mind wanders to random thoughts like the chicken in the freezer or the shoe rack—do those thoughts feel like they're just popping up randomly and grabbing your attention, or do they feel more like you're actively worried or anxious about those things and can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s definitely not worry. I’m not sitting there stressing out about the chicken or the shoe rack, you know? It’s not like my heart is racing or I’m panicking that dinner is going to be ruined or anything like that. It’s honestly just... random. Like, completely out of left field. \n\nI could be in the middle of typing up the minutes from the weekly staff meeting—which, by the way, takes forever because they always go off on these ridiculous tangent discussions about the breakroom fridge policy that I somehow have to summarize into professional bullet points—and right in the middle of typing, I’ll just suddenly think about... I don't know, the fact that I need to buy more of those little velvet hangers for my closet. \n\nAnd I’m not anxious about the hangers! I have plenty of hangers right now. It’s just a thought that literally drops into my brain from nowhere, almost like a pop-up ad on a website. You know when you're reading an article and suddenly there's an ad for shoes blocking the whole screen? You didn't click on anything to make it appear, it’s just there. And then once it’s there, I just kind of follow it. Like, oh, velvet hangers, I should check if Target has them on sale, maybe I’ll get the blush pink ones this time instead of the black ones because the black ones leave a little fuzz on my white blouses... and before I know it, I’m mentally reorganizing my wardrobe instead of finishing the meeting minutes. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm never actually worried or anxious about the things that pop into my head. It’s more like my brain just gets bored with the email or the spreadsheet and decides to throw a totally random thought at me just to see what happens. It’s not this heavy, stressful thing at all. Just completely random noise!\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your desk trying to work, or even when you're just relaxing at home, do you find yourself fidgeting a lot—like tapping your pen, bouncing your leg, or just having trouble sitting still—or do you feel restless and like you need to be moving around?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I definitely do that, especially when I'm stuck sitting in one place for an extended period. Like, going back to that evening QuickBooks class I was telling you about earlier—those classes were three hours long, twice a week, which is honestly just an eternity after you've already worked a full eight-hour shift at the front desk. And the chairs in that community college room were those terrible hard plastic ones that are attached to the little desks, you know the kind? They are just completely unforgiving on your lower back. \n\nAnyway, during those longer classes, I would always notice myself doing exactly what you're talking about. I’d be sitting there, really trying my absolute hardest to sit perfectly still and pay attention to the instructor—because QuickBooks is really not intuitive, let me tell you, and if you miss one step in the reconciliation process, the whole balance sheet is off—but my body just wouldn't cooperate. \n\nI'd catch myself clicking my pen over and over. Usually, I use these nice Pilot G2 gel pens, the retractable ones, and they have a very distinct, kind of loud click. The guy sitting next to me actually gave me a look once because I was doing it so much without even realizing it! And if I force myself to put the pen down and stop clicking it, then within two minutes I realize I'm bouncing my foot. Just rapidly tapping my heel on the linoleum floor under the desk. \n\nAnd the shifting! I am constantly shifting around in the chair. I’ll cross my right leg over my left, then a few minutes later I just have to uncross them and cross the left over the right, then I’m leaning on my left elbow, then I’m sitting up straight, then I’m slouching again. It’s like my body just physically rejects the idea of staying in one position. Even when I am genuinely trying to sit still and focus, I just can't seem to stop the little movements. I don't necessarily feel this overwhelming, bursting urge to get up and run a marathon or anything, it's not like I need to pace the hallways, but it's just this constant, low-level need to be doing *something* with my hands or my feet. Even at my desk at work, if I'm on a long phone call with a vendor, I'm usually swiveling side to side in my office chair or unbending a paperclip while I talk. I just can't seem to stay totally still!\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were in elementary school or middle school, before you started this job as an administrative assistant—do you remember having similar kinds of trouble back then? Like, did your teachers ever mention that you had difficulty paying attention in class, or did you find yourself fidgeting a lot even as a kid?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yes. Going all the way back to elementary school, it was... well, it was a whole ordeal, honestly. My mom and I actually joke about it now, but back then it was incredibly frustrating for her. I don't know if my teachers specifically used the word fidgeting on my report cards, but they definitely noticed I was struggling to keep things together and stay on track. \n\nI was absolutely that kid who lost her homework in her backpack. I had this bright purple Lisa Frank backpack—the one with the neon dolphins, I loved that thing so much—but the inside of it was just a complete disaster zone. It was like a black hole. I would do my math worksheet at the kitchen table, put it in my folder, put the folder in the backpack, and then somehow by nine in the morning the next day, it was just gone. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be digging through old wrappers, broken crayons, and just mounds of crumpled paper at the bottom of the bag, and I just couldn't find it. \n\nAnd permission slips! Oh my gosh, forget about it. I was notorious for forgetting permission slips. We would have these field trips, like I remember this one specific trip to the natural history museum in third grade, and the teacher had handed out the bright yellow slips a week in advance. I stuffed it in my desk, or my bag, and completely forgot it existed until the morning we were supposed to get on the bus. My mom actually had to leave her job, drive all the way to the school, and sign a spare copy in the front office so I wouldn't have to stay behind in the library. She would always find the original slip like three weeks later, completely crinkled up under a squished juice box.\n\nIt was the exact same story with any kind of assignment, too. If we had a project, I just couldn't stay focused on it. I needed my mother, or my teacher—like Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, she was such a saint—they literally had to remind me step by step to finish projects. If they just told me to build a diorama by Friday, I would stare at the shoebox and get completely overwhelmed or distracted by the glue sticks. My mom would have to sit right next to me and tell me to paint the background first, and then tell me to wait for it to dry, and then remind me to cut out the trees. If she didn't walk me through it and constantly remind me of the very next step, I would just wander off or start playing with the modeling clay and completely forget what I was supposed to be doing. So, yeah, this definitely isn't a new thing for me, it's been happening since I was little."
